\displaystyle\widetilde{\chi}_{m-n_{k^{\prime}}}(n)\otimes\widetilde{\chi}_{m^{\prime}-n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{m^{\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{m-n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\psi^{\ast}_{m-n_{k^{\prime}},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},m^{\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}},m-n^{\prime}}(n)b(n)\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

where
\psi_{m-n_{k^{\prime}},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},m^{\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}},m-n^{\prime}}
is a degree
(d-2)
nilsequence. Now by Cauchy–Schwarz, duplicating the variable
m
and denoting the copies by
m,m^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg\lVert\mathbb{E}_{m,m^{\prime\prime}\in I_{1},m^{\prime}\in I_{2},n_{k^{\prime}}\in I_{3},n^{\prime}\in I_{4},n_{k-k^{\prime}}\in[N]^{k-k^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\chi}_{m-n_{k^{\prime}}}(n)\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{m^{\prime\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{m-n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})}\otimes\widetilde{\chi}_{m^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cdot\psi^{\ast}_{m-n_{k^{\prime}},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime},m^{\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}},m-n^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime}-n,m^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}(n)\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

Note that every term not involving
m
was removed using appropriate boundedness. Now we may Pigeonhole on
m^{\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}}=t
and deduce

\displaystyle\bigg\lVert\mathbb{E}_{m,m^{\prime\prime}\in I_{1},n_{k^{\prime}}\in I_{3},n^{\prime}\in I_{4},n_{k-k^{\prime}}\in[N]^{k-k^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\chi}_{m-n_{k^{\prime}}}(n)\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{m^{\prime\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{m-n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})}\otimes\widetilde{\chi}_{m^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\psi^{\ast}_{m-n_{k^{\prime}},m-n^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}},m^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}}(n)\cdot\mathbbm{1}[n_{k^{\prime}}+t\in I_{2}]\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

Let
m^{\prime\prime}-n_{k^{\prime}}=h_{1}
,
m-n^{\prime}=h_{2}
,
m-n_{k^{\prime}}=h_{3}
, and
m^{\prime\prime}-n^{\prime}=h_{4}
(abusively). We have

\displaystyle\bigg\lVert

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{n\in[N]^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}\\
h_{i}\in[\pm N]^{k^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{1}}(n)\otimes\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{2}}(n+h_{1}-h_{4})\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{3}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{4}}(n+h_{1}-h_{4})}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\chi_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}}(n)\cdot\mathbbm{1}[n_{k^{\prime}}+h_{3},n_{k^{\prime}}+h_{1}\in I_{1},n_{k^{\prime}}\in I_{3},n_{k^{\prime}}+h_{1}-h_{4}\in I_{4},n_{k^{\prime}}+t\in I_{2}]\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

where
\chi_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}}(n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{d-2}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
.

Therefore, by the triangle inequality we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

{}_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}\\
h_{i}\in[\pm N]^{k^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\bigg\lVert\mathbb{E}_{n\in[N]^{k}}\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{1}}(n)\otimes\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{2}}(n+h_{1}-h_{4})\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{3}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\widetilde{\chi}_{h_{4}}(n+h_{1}-h_{4})}

\displaystyle\qquad\chi_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}}(n)\cdot\mathbbm{1}[n_{k^{\prime}}+h_{3},n_{k^{\prime}}+h_{1}\in I_{1},n_{k^{\prime}}\in I_{3},n_{k^{\prime}}+h_{1}-h_{4}\in I_{4},n_{k^{\prime}}+t\in I_{2}]\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\gtrsim\epsilon(\delta).

As the last term is a
\vec{h}
-dependent indicator, this gives the desired estimate.

Next, by
[
38
, Lemma C.5]
, there exist
\chi_{h}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(1,1,\dots,1)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
for which
\chi_{h}(n)
is
(M(\delta),m(\delta))
-equivalent to
\chi_{h}^{\prime\prime}(n,n,\dots,n)
. We invoking Lemma
2.10
we may replace
\chi_{h}
with
\chi_{h}^{\prime\prime}
at the cost of a lower degree nilsequence with appropriate complexity parameters and by the Cauchy-Schwarz-Gowers inequality (in particular Cauchy-Schwarzing away the factors of
f_{i,h}
), we see that

\|\|\chi_{h_{1}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)}\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{4}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)}\cdot\psi_{h_{1},\dots,h_{4}}\|_{U(e_{1}[N],\dots,e_{\ell}[N])}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

Unfortunately,
\chi^{\prime}_{h}(n)
is not approximately linear in
n
; rather, it is only approximately
multilinear
in
n
. (Note that if
\chi_{h}
is degree zero in
e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}
, then taking a difference in the Box norm yields a strictly lower degree nilsequence, which is undesirable; this is why this slightly strange condition of the lemma is present.) This can be remedied by taking more and more differences. Hence,

\|\|\chi_{h_{1}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)}\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{4}}^{\prime\prime}(n,\dots,n)}\cdot\psi_{h_{1},\dots,h_{4}}\|_{U(e_{1}[N],\dots,e_{\ell}[N],[N]^{k},\dots,[N]^{k})}\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

where we add sufficiently many differences so that the number of differences is
d-1
. By expanding out the box norm, using multilinear properties of nilcharacters, and pigeonholing in the sufficient variables, we find that

\|\mathbb{E}_{n\in([N]^{k})^{d}}(\chi_{h_{1}}^{\prime\prime}(n)\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}^{\prime\prime}(n)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}^{\prime\prime}(n)\otimes\chi_{h_{4}}^{\prime\prime}(n)})^{\otimes q}\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(n)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

for some
q=O_{K}(1)
. This, combined with
[
38
, Lemma C.3]
, implies that
\chi_{h_{1}}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\chi_{h_{4}}^{\prime\prime}}
is degree
\leq d-2
, and since
\chi_{h}
is equivalent to
n\mapsto\chi_{h}^{\prime\prime}(n,n,\dots,n)
, we find that
\chi_{h_{1}}\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}\otimes\chi_{h_{4}}}
is degree
\leq d-2
. This gives the desired conclusion.
∎

7.
Deduction of Proposition
5.2

We will now deduce Proposition
5.2
. The individual steps will follow
[
38
]
rather closely, with the biggest difference is that of the construction of the nilmanifold in the proof of Proposition
5.2
.

7.1.
A sunflower-type argument

We prove the following lemma, which is essentially the same as
[
38
, Lemma 6.1]
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\epsilon>0
,
(r\leq d),D,D^{\prime}\leq K
be positive integers, and
d>1
. Let
\chi_{h}(x)=F(g_{h}(x)\Gamma)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(d,r)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),D,M(\delta))
with
G_{(d,r)}
frequency
\eta
and
\psi_{\vec{h}}=\psi_{\vec{h}}(g_{\vec{h}}(n)\Gamma^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Nil}^{d-1}(M(\delta),m(\delta),D,1)
. Let
H\subseteq[N]^{D^{\prime}}
be a subset of size at least
\delta N^{D^{\prime}}
and suppose for at least
\delta|H|^{3}
many additive quadruples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}
, we have

\|\mathbb{E}_{x\in[N]^{D}}\chi_{h_{1}}(x)\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}(x)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}(x)\otimes\chi_{h_{4}}(x)}\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(x)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

Then there exists a set
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
of size at least
\epsilon(\delta)|H|
such that for any
h\in H^{\prime}
, we have the following.

•

There exists a collection of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces
V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}\leq V_{i}\leq G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}
which are
M(\delta)
-rational with respect to
\exp(\mathcal{X}_{i})
for each
i
;

•

We have a factorization
g_{h}=\epsilon_{h}g_{h}^{\prime}\gamma_{h}
with
\epsilon_{h}

(M(\delta),\vec{N})
-smooth and
\gamma_{h}

M(\delta)
-rational;

•

For
\vec{i}
with
1\leq|\vec{i}|\leq\ell^{\prime}-1
and
h,h_{1},h_{2}\in H^{\prime}
, we have

\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h})\in V_{|\vec{i}|},\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}})-\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{2}})\in V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}};

•

For integers
i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{r}=\ell^{\prime}-1
, and
v_{i_{\ell}}\in V_{i_{\ell}}
such that for at least two distinct indices
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
we have
v_{i_{\ell_{1}}}\in V_{i_{\ell_{1}},\mathrm{Dep}}
and
v_{i_{\ell_{2}}}\in V_{i_{\ell_{2}},\mathrm{Dep}}
, we have for any
r-1
-fold commutator,

\eta([v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i_{r}}])=0.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as in
[
38
, Lemma 6.1]
and we repeat large portions of it here essentially verbatim. (The only meaningful difference is in step 5 of this proof.) First, we note that the statement of the lemma is trivial for
r=1
as the only content of the lemma is the third bullet point, and there we may simply take
V_{i}=V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}=G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}
.

Step 1: Setup for invoking equidistribution theory.

By hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\bigg\lVert\mathbb{E}\bigg[\chi_{h_{1}}(n)\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}(n+h_{1}-h_{4})\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{4}}(n+h_{1}-h_{4})}\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(g_{\vec{h}}(n)\Gamma^{\prime})\bigg]\bigg\rVert_{\infty}\geq\delta.

for at least
\rho
fraction of additive quadruples
h_{1}+h_{2}=h_{3}+h_{4}
. Let
\chi_{h}(n)=F_{h}(g_{h}(n)\Gamma)
. By pigeonholing in the nilmanifolds of complexity
M(\delta)
and using a similar partition of unity argument as in Proposition
4.1
, we may assume that
G_{\mathrm{Error}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{Error}}
,
G_{h}/\Gamma_{h}
, and
F_{h}
are independent of
h
. We may additionally assume that
g_{\vec{h}}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Error}}}
via
[
34
, Lemma 2.1]
.

We now consider the group
\widetilde{G}=G\times G\times G\times G\times G_{\mathrm{Error}}
.
\widetilde{G}
may naturally be given a degree-rank
(d,r)
product filtration and Mal’cev basis, with
G_{\mathrm{Error}}
may be given a degree-rank
(d-1,d-1)
filtration (note that in our definition, we have
(d-1,d-1)<(d,r)
). Note that
F(x_{1}\Gamma)\otimes\overline{F(x_{2}\Gamma)\otimes F(x_{3}\Gamma)}\otimes F(x_{4}\Gamma)\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(x_{5}\Gamma_{\mathrm{Error}})
has a vertical frequency
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}=(\eta,\eta,-\eta,-\eta,0)
.

For the sake of convenience, we set

g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}(n)=(g_{h_{1}}(n),g_{h_{2}}(n),g_{h_{3}}(n),g_{h_{4}}(n),g_{\vec{h}}(n)).

Step 2: Invoking equidistribution theory.

By applying
[
38
, Corollary 5.2]
(since
\eta
is nonzero), there exists a
M(\delta)
-rational subgroup
J=J_{\vec{h}}
of
\widetilde{G}
such that
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}(J\cap\widetilde{G}_{(s-1,r)})=0
and such that

g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}=\varepsilon_{\vec{h}}\cdot\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}\cdot\gamma_{\vec{h}}

where:

•

\varepsilon_{\vec{h}}(0)=\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}(0)=\gamma_{\vec{h}}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{\widetilde{G}}
;

•

\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}
takes values in
J
;

•

\gamma_{\vec{h}}
is
M(\delta)
-rational (with respect to the lattice
\Gamma\times\Gamma\times\Gamma\times\Gamma\times\Gamma_{\mathrm{Error}}
);

•

\varepsilon
is
(M(\delta),\vec{N})
-smooth.

By pigeonholing to a subset of additive quadruples of density
M(\delta)
we may in fact assume that the group
J
is independent of
\vec{h}
under consideration. Recall the definition of
\mathrm{Horiz}_{i}
, which we define below.

Definition 7.2

(
i
th horizontal component)
.

Let
G
be given a degree-rank
(d^{\prime},r^{\prime})
filtration. We define
\mathrm{Horiz}_{i}(G)=G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}
.

We define

J_{i}^{\prime}:=(J\cap\widetilde{G}_{(i,1)})/(J\cap\widetilde{G}_{(i,2)}),\qquad J_{i}:=\tau_{i}(J_{i}^{\prime})

where
\tau_{i}\colon\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)^{\otimes 4}\times\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G_{\mathrm{Error}})\to\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)^{\otimes 4}
is the natural projection map to the four-fold product. Since
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}(J\cap\widetilde{G}_{(s-1,r)})=0
, we have

\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([J_{i_{1}}^{\prime},\ldots,J_{i_{r}}^{\prime}])=0

for
i_{1}^{\prime}+\ldots+i_{r}^{\prime}=d-1
where the commutator bracket is taken with respect to
\widetilde{G}
and
[\cdot,\ldots,\cdot]
denotes any possible
(r-1)
-fold commutator bracket.

Note that since
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}
vanishes on
(G_{\mathrm{Error}})_{(d,r)}
we in fact have

\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([J_{i_{1}},\ldots,J_{i_{r}}])=0

where we abusively descend
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}
to
G^{\otimes 4}
.

Step 3: Furstenberg–Weiss commutator argument.

We now perform the crucial Furstenberg–Weiss commutator argument to find
V_{i}
and
V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}
. Given
T\subseteq[4]
, we define
\pi_{T}((v_{1},\ldots,v_{4}))=(v_{i})_{i\in T}
with the coordinates represented in increasing order of index.

We define

\displaystyle\pi_{123}(J_{i})^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\pi_{123}(J_{i})\cap\{(v,0,0)\colon v\in\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)\},

\displaystyle\pi_{124}(J_{i})^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\pi_{124}(J_{i})\cap\{(v,0,0)\colon v\in\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)\}.

Note that
\pi_{123}(J_{i})^{\ast}
and
\pi_{124}(J_{i})^{\ast}
may (abusively) be viewed as subspaces of
\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)
. The crucial claim is that

\eta([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}])=0

if
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=s-1
, each
v_{i_{\ell}}\in\pi_{1}(J_{i_{\ell}})
, and for two distinct indices
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
we have that
v_{i_{\ell_{1}}}\in\pi_{123}(J_{i_{\ell_{1}}})^{\ast}
and
v_{i_{\ell_{2}}}\in\pi_{124}(J_{i_{\ell_{2}}})^{\ast}
. Note that
\eta
lives on
G
and the commutator brackets are taken with respect to
G
, not
G^{\otimes 4}
.

Note that an element
v_{i}\in\pi_{1}(J_{i_{\ell}})
lifts to an element
\widetilde{v_{i_{\ell}}}
of the form
(v_{i_{\ell}},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\in\operatorname{Horiz}_{i_{\ell}}(G)^{\otimes 4}
. Furthermore note that
v_{i_{\ell}}\in\pi_{123}(J_{i_{\ell}})
“lifts” to an element
\widetilde{v_{i_{\ell}}}
of the form
(v_{i_{\ell}},0,0,\cdot)\in\operatorname{Horiz}_{i_{\ell}}(G)^{\otimes 4}
while
v_{i_{\ell}}\in\pi_{124}(J_{i_{\ell}})
lifts to an element
\widetilde{v_{i_{\ell}}}
of the form
(v_{i_{\ell}},0,\cdot,0)\in\operatorname{Horiz}_{i_{\ell}}(G)^{\otimes 4}
.

Given the above setup, we have

[\widetilde{v_{i_{1}}},\ldots,\widetilde{v_{i_{{r}}}}]=([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{{r}}}],\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{G}).

To see this note that the iterated commutator of elements in
G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times G
(with any elements in
G^{\otimes 4}
) remains in the subgroup
G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times G
; an analogous fact holds true for
G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}
. Since we assumed that our commutator contains elements in both
G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times G
and
G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}
, the commutator must in fact live in
G\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}
, and the first coordinates of the desired commutators is trivially seen to match.

Recalling that we have

\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([J_{i_{1}},\ldots,J_{i_{r}}])=0,

and noting that
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}
descends to
\eta
on the subgroup
G_{(s-1,r)}\times\mathrm{Id}_{G}^{\otimes 3}
, we have

\eta([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{{r}}}])=0

as claimed.

Step 4: Finding
(h_{2},h_{3})
and
(h_{2}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime})
which extend to many “good”
h_{1}
.

Recall that we are looking at the at least
\epsilon(\delta)
fraction of additive quadruples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}\subseteq[N]^{4D^{\prime}}
which are such that
\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}
lives on a specified subgroup
J
. Call this set of quadruples
\mathcal{S}
.
By the pigeonhole principle, there are at least
\epsilon(\delta)N^{D^{\prime}}
many
h_{1}\in[N]^{D^{\prime}}
which extend to at least
\epsilon(\delta)N^{2D^{\prime}}
quadruples in
\mathcal{S}
. Thus there are at least
\epsilon(\delta)N^{5D^{\prime}}
pairs of additive tuples of the form

(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}),~(h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime},h_{1}+h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{4}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}.

By averaging, there exists a pair of pairs
(h_{2},h_{3})

and
(h_{2}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime})
such that there are at least
\epsilon(\delta)N^{D^{\prime}}
many
h_{1}\in[N]^{D^{\prime}}
which live in such additive tuples. We fix such a pair of pairs and define
\mathcal{T}
to denote the set of
h_{1}\in[N]^{D^{\prime}}
such that
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3})\in\mathcal{S}
and
(h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime},h_{1}+h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{4}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
.

Step 5: Extracting coefficient data.

Consider
h_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
and define

h^{123}=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}),\quad h^{124}=(h_{1},h_{2}^{\prime},h_{1}+h_{2}^{\prime}-h_{4}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime}).

Recall
\mathcal{X}_{i}=(\mathcal{X}\cap\log(G_{(i,1)}))/\log(G_{(i,2)})
and assign the basis
\exp(\mathcal{X}_{i})
to
G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}
(viewed as a vector space).
Finally we assign the basis
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\bigcup_{X_{i}\in\exp(\mathcal{X}_{i})}\{(X_{i},0,0),(0,X_{i},0),(0,0,X_{i})\}
to
(G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)})^{\otimes 3}
. Write

J_{i,123}=\{(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}):v_{1}^{i}(g_{1})+v^{i}_{2}(g_{2})+v^{i}_{3}(g_{3})=0\forall(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in S_{i,123}\}

J_{i,124}=\{(g_{1},g_{2},g_{4}):v_{1}^{i}(g_{1})+v_{i}^{2}(g_{2})+v^{i}_{4}(g_{4})=0\forall(v_{1},v_{2},v_{4})\in S_{i,124}\}.

Fix
i
and let the projection to
h_{1}
component of elements in
S_{i,123}\cup S_{i,124}
be
(\tilde{v}^{1},\dots,\tilde{v}^{r})
and suppose without a loss of generality that
\tilde{v}^{i}
are linearly independent. We take
V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}=\pi_{1}(J_{i,123})\cap\pi_{2}(J_{i,124})
, and
V_{i}=\pi_{1}(J_{i})
. By our analysis in Step 3, it follows that for this choice of vector spaces satisfies the fourth bullet point of the lemma.

Let us also assume without a loss of generality that
\tilde{v}^{1},\dots,\tilde{v}^{r}
are Gaussian eliminated and (after possibly reordering the variables) are in row-reduced echelon form. This can be done while keeping similar bounds for these elements. Now let
\vec{i}
be a
D
-dimensional integer vector. Noticing that
h_{2},h_{3},h_{2}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime}
are fixed, there exists fixed
h
-independent phases
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}
such that for each
h\in\mathcal{T}
, we have for some integer
T_{h}\leq M(\delta)
,

\tilde{v}^{j}(\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}))+\alpha_{j}\in T_{h}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}+M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|}.

Pigeonholing in
T
, we have for some
H^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
of size at least
\epsilon(\delta)|H|
such that

\tilde{v}^{j}(\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}))+\alpha_{j}\in T^{-1}\mathbb{Z}+M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|}.

This shows that for
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
,

\tilde{v}^{j}(\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}g_{h^{\prime}}^{-1}))\in T^{-1}\mathbb{Z}+M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|}.

In addition, since
\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}},g_{h_{2}},g_{h_{3}},g_{h_{4}})\in J_{|\vec{i}|}
, it follows that
\mathrm{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}})\in\pi_{1}(J_{|\vec{i}|})=V_{|\vec{i}|}
. Applying the factorization theorem to
g_{h}g_{h^{\prime}}^{-1}
, we have obtained the third bullet point of the lemma. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

7.2.
Linearization

We will now refine the nilsequence so that it admits bracket linear behavior in
h
. This is essentially
[
38
, Lemma 9.1]
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\delta>0
and
d,r,D,D^{\prime},K
be positive integers with
d,r,D,D^{\prime},\leq K
. For
h\in[N]^{D}
, let
\chi_{h}(x)=F(g_{h}(x)\Gamma)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{d}(M(\delta),m(\delta),D,M(\delta))
be a degree-rank
(d,r)
nilcharacter
G_{(d,r)}
-vertical frequency
\eta
and
\psi_{\vec{h}}=\psi_{\vec{h}}(g_{\vec{h}}(n)\Gamma^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Nil}^{d-1}(M(\delta),m(\delta),D,M(\delta))
a nilsequence on
G_{\mathrm{Error}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{Error}}
. Let
H\subseteq[N]^{D^{\prime}}
be a subset of size at least
\delta N^{D^{\prime}}
and suppose for
\delta|H|^{3}
many additive quadruples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in H^{4}
we have

\|\mathbb{E}_{x\in[N]^{D}}\chi_{h_{1}}(x)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{2}}(x)\otimes\chi_{h_{3}}(x)}\otimes\chi_{h_{4}}(x)\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(x)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

Then there exists a set
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
with
|H^{\prime}|\geq\epsilon(\delta)|H|
such that for any
h\in H^{\prime}
, we have the following.

•

For each
h\in H^{\prime}
, there exists a factorization
g_{h}=\epsilon_{h}g_{h}^{\prime}\gamma_{h}
with
\epsilon_{h}

(M(\delta),N^{D})
-smooth,
\gamma_{h}
is
M(\delta)
-rational;

•

There exists a collection
\mathcal{X}_{i,*}
,
\mathcal{X}_{i,\mathrm{lin}}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{i,\mathrm{pet}}
which are
M(\delta)
-rational combinations of elements in
(\mathcal{X}\cap G_{(i,1)})/G_{(i,2)}
.

•

There is a collection of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces
W_{i,\ast},W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}},W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}\leqslant G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}
for each
i
;

•

If
W_{i}:=W_{i,\ast}+W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}
then
\dim(W_{i})=\dim(W_{i,\ast})+\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})+\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}})
, i.e., the three spaces are linearly disjoint;

•

There exist bases
\mathcal{X}_{i,\ast}
,
\mathcal{X}_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}
of the corresponding spaces which are composed of
M(\delta)
-rational combinations of elements of
(\mathcal{X}\cap G_{(i,1)})/G_{(i,2)}
;

•

For
1\leq|\vec{i}|\leq d
and
h,h_{1},h_{2}\in H^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\in W_{|\vec{i}|,\ast}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}=W_{|\vec{i}|},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}}^{\prime})-\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{2}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\in W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Proj}_{W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{Z_{|\vec{i}|,j}\in\mathcal{X}_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}}\bigg(\gamma_{\vec{i},j}+\sum_{k=1}^{m^{\ast}}\alpha_{\vec{i},j,k}\{\beta_{k}\cdot h\}\bigg)Z_{|\vec{i}|,j},

with
m^{\ast}\leq m(\delta)
and
\beta_{k}\in(1/N^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}^{D^{\prime}}
where
N^{\prime}
is a prime in
[100N,200N]
;

•

For any integers
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=d
, suppose that
v_{i_{j}}\in V_{i_{j}}
for all
j
. If for at least one index
\ell_{0}
we have
v_{i_{\ell_{0}}}\in W_{i_{\ell_{0}},\mathrm{Pet}}
, then if
w
is any
(r-1)
-fold commutator of
v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}
we have

\eta(w)=0.

Furthermore, if instead for at least two indices
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
we have
v_{i_{\ell_{1}}}\in W_{i_{\ell_{1}},\mathrm{Lin}}
and
v_{i_{\ell_{2}}}\in W_{i_{\ell_{2}},\mathrm{Lin}}
, then if
w
is any
(r-1)
-fold commutator of
v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}
we have

\eta(w)=0.

Proof.

Again, much of this proof will essentially repeat the proof of
[
38
, Lemma 9.1]
essentially verbatim. For the majority of the proof we will assume
d\geq 2
; we indicate the minor changes required for
d=1
for the end of the proof. Note that the case when
\eta
is trivial follows via taking
W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}=G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}
,
W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}
and
W_{i,\ast}
to be trivial; therefore we may assume that
\eta
is nontrivial for the remainder of the proof.

Step 1: Applying Lemma
7.1
and linear-algebraic setup.

Applying Lemma
7.1
, we obtain a factorization
g_{h}=\epsilon_{h}g^{\prime}_{h}\gamma_{h}
for
h\in H^{\prime}
of size at least
\epsilon(\delta)|H|
such that
\epsilon_{h}
is
(M(\delta),\vec{N})
-smooth,
\gamma_{h}
is
M(\delta)
-rational, and
g_{h}^{\prime}
satisfies the conclusions of the lemma. By restricting in a progression of the period of
\gamma
, Fourier expanding the progression (via e.g.,
[
38
, Lemma 7.1]
), pigeonholing in a phase
e(\Theta_{h}n)
, and absorbing the phase in
\psi_{h}
(and here we are using the fact that
d>1
), we may assume that (after possibly also conjugating
g_{h}^{\prime}
by a constant
\epsilon_{0}
)

\|\mathbb{E}_{x\in[N]^{D}}\chi_{h_{1}}^{\prime}(x)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{2}}^{\prime}(x)\otimes\chi_{h_{3}}^{\prime}(x)}\otimes\chi_{h_{4}}^{\prime}(x)\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(x)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

where if
\chi_{h}(n)=F(g_{h}(n)\Gamma)
,
\chi_{h}^{\prime}(n)=\widetilde{F}(g_{h}^{\prime}(n)\Gamma)
with
\widetilde{F}=F(\epsilon_{0}\cdot)
for some choice of
\epsilon_{0}
with
|\psi_{\mathcal{X}}(\epsilon_{0})|\leq 1
. We shall now abusively relabel
g_{h}^{\prime}
as
g_{h}
,
H^{\prime}
as
H
, and
\chi_{h}^{\prime}
as
\chi_{h}
.

We now require some additional linear algebraic setup for the proof. We define

\displaystyle V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(w_{i,1},\ldots,w_{i,\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})})

\displaystyle V_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(w_{i,1},\ldots,w_{i,\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})},w_{i,\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})+1},\ldots,w_{i,\dim(V_{i})}),

\displaystyle G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(w_{i,1},\ldots,w_{i,\dim(\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G))}).

Given
v\in V_{i}
, there is a unique linear combination

v=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim(V_{i})}\alpha_{j}w_{i,j}.

We define

P_{i}v=\sum_{j=\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})+1}^{\dim(V_{i})}\alpha_{j}w_{i,j},\qquad Q_{i}v=\sum_{j=1}^{\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})}\alpha_{j}w_{i,j}.

By construction
P_{i}^{2}=P_{i}
,
Q_{i}^{2}=Q_{i}
,
Q_{i}(V_{i})\cap P_{i}(V_{i})=0
, and
P_{i}v+Q_{i}v=v
for
v\in V_{i}
. We also (abusively) extend the operator
P_{i}
to
V_{i}^{\otimes\ell}
and
(G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)})^{\otimes 4}
in the obvious manners by acting on each copy of
V_{i}
separately (and zeroing out basis elements
w_{i,\dim(V_{i})+1},\ldots,w_{i,\dim(\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G))}
).

Step 2: Invoking equidistribution theory.

We thus have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbb{E}[\chi_{h_{1}}(n)\otimes\chi_{h_{2}}(n)\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{3}}(n)}\otimes\overline{\chi_{h_{4}}(n)}\cdot\psi_{\vec{h}}(g_{\vec{h}}(n)\Gamma^{\prime})]\rVert_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

for a
\epsilon(\delta)
density of additive tuples. We define
G_{\mathrm{Error}}
,
\widetilde{G}
, and
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}
as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
and as before we may assume that
g_{\vec{h}}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Error}}}
. Define

g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}(n)=(g_{h_{1}}(n),g_{h_{2}}(n),g_{h_{3}}(n),g_{h_{4}}(n),g_{\vec{h}}(n)).

By applying
[
38
, Corollary 5.5]
, we have

g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}=\varepsilon_{\vec{h}}\cdot\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}\cdot\gamma_{\vec{h}}

with

•

\varepsilon_{\vec{h}}(0)=\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}(0)=\gamma_{\vec{h}}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{\widetilde{G}}
;

•

\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}
takes values in
K
;

•

\gamma_{\vec{h}}
is
M(\delta)
-rational;

•

\varepsilon
is
(M(\delta),\vec{N})
-smooth

where
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}(K\cap\widetilde{G}_{(d-1,r)})=0
and
K
is a
M(\delta)
-rational subgroup of
\widetilde{G}
. By passing to a subset of additive quadruples of density
\epsilon(\delta)
we may in fact assume that the group
K
is independent of
\vec{h}
under consideration.

Step 3: Linear algebra deductions from equidistribution theory.

Note that at present the subgroup
K
does not account for the deductions given in Lemma
7.1
; these initial deductions are designed essentially to account for this. Let
\tau_{i}\colon\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)^{\otimes 4}\times\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G_{\mathrm{Error}})\to\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(G)^{\otimes 4}
be the natural projection to the four-fold product. We define the following set of vector spaces:

\displaystyle R_{i}

\displaystyle:=\{(Q_{i}v_{1},Q_{i}v_{2},Q_{i}v_{3},Q_{i}v_{4})\in V_{i}^{\otimes 4}\colon Q_{i}v_{1}+Q_{i}v_{2}-Q_{i}v_{3}-Q_{i}v_{4}=0\},

\displaystyle K_{i}

\displaystyle:=\tau_{i}(K\cap\widetilde{G}_{(i,1)}~\mathrm{mod}~\widetilde{G}_{(i,2)}),

\displaystyle S_{i}

\displaystyle:=\{(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})\in V_{i}^{\otimes 4}\colon P_{i}v_{1}=P_{i}v_{2}=P_{i}v_{3}=P_{i}v_{4}\},

\displaystyle K_{i,1}

\displaystyle:=K_{i}\cap S_{i},

\displaystyle\widetilde{K_{i}}

\displaystyle:=K_{i,1}+R_{i},

\displaystyle L_{i}

\displaystyle:=\pi_{1}(\widetilde{K_{i}}\cap\{(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})\in V_{i}^{\otimes 4}\colon Q_{i}v_{2}=Q_{i}v_{3}=Q_{i}v_{4}=0\})+Q_{i}(V_{i}).

By inspection, we have
R_{i}\leqslant S_{i}
hence
\widetilde{K_{i}}\leqslant S_{i}
. Note that

\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([K_{i_{1},1},\ldots,K_{i_{r}^{\ast},1}])=0

whenever one has that
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=d-1
and
[\cdot,\ldots,\cdot]
denotes any possible
(d-1)
-fold commutator bracket. This is a consequence of the fact that
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}(K\cap\widetilde{G}_{(d-1,r)})=0
and noting that
K_{i,1}\leqslant K_{i}
. Note that we are implicitly using that
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}
is trivial on
G_{\mathrm{Error}}
as well, and we abusively descend
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}
to
G^{\otimes 4}
.

We now claim that

\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}])=0

if
v_{i_{\ell}}\in S_{i_{\ell}}
for all
\ell
and there is at least one index
j
such that
v_{i_{j}}\in R_{i_{j}}
.

To prove this, note by the final bullet point of Lemma
7.1
and multilinearity that

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{r}}])

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([P_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}},\ldots,P_{i_{r}}v_{i_{r}}])+\sum_{k=1}^{r}\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([P_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}},\ldots,Q_{i_{k}}v_{i_{k}},\ldots,P_{i_{r}}v_{i_{r}}])

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([P_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}},\ldots,Q_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}},\ldots,P_{i_{r}}v_{i_{r}}])=0.

The first equality uses that every bracket with at least two
Q_{i_{k}}v_{i_{k}}
has two
V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}
terms so is
0
, the second equality uses
P_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}}=0
, and the third equality follows by noting that

P_{i}v_{i}\in\{(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})\in V_{i}^{\otimes 4}\colon P_{i}v_{1}=P_{i}v_{2}=P_{i}v_{3}=P_{i}v_{4},Q_{i}v_{1}=Q_{i}v_{2}=Q_{i}v_{3}=Q_{i}v_{4}=0\}

and
\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}=(\eta,\eta,-\eta,-\eta)
.
Now, we may ultimately deduce

\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([\widetilde{K_{i_{1}}},\ldots,\widetilde{K_{i_{r}}}])=0

because
\widetilde{K_{i}}=K_{i,1}+R_{i}
and
R_{i},K_{i,1}\leqslant S_{i}
.

Finally, let
\pi_{T}
for
T\subseteq[4]
is as in the proof of Lemma
7.1
(namely, an appropriate projection map). We have

\pi_{1}(\widetilde{K_{i}})\leqslant L_{i}.

This follows because if

((Qv_{1},Pv_{1}),(Qv_{2},Pv_{2}),(Qv_{3},Pv_{3}),(Qv_{4},Pv_{4}))\in\widetilde{K_{i}}

then

((Q(v_{1}+v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}),Pv_{1}),(0,Pv_{2}),(0,Pv_{3}),(0,Pv_{4}))\in\widetilde{K_{i}}.

Step 4: Constructing a decomposition of
Q_{i}(V_{i})
.

We will now decompose
Q_{i}(V_{i})=V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}
into a pair of subspaces. On one of these subspaces we will deduce an improved vanishing for the commutator while on the other subspace we will deduce an approximate linearity for
\operatorname{Taylor}_{i}(g_{h})
. Let

L_{i}^{\ast}=\{(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})\in S_{i}\colon Pv_{1}=0,v_{2}=v_{3}=v_{4}=0\}\cap\widetilde{K_{i}}.

Note that
L_{i}^{\ast}
may abusively be viewed as a subspace of
V_{i}
(instead of
V_{i}^{\otimes 4}
) and under this identification
L_{i}^{\ast}\leqslant Q_{i}(V_{i})=V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}\leqslant L_{i}
.

The key claim in our analysis is if
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=d-1
,
v_{i_{\ell}}\in L_{i_{\ell}}
for all indices
\ell
, and
v_{i_{j}}\in L_{i_{j}}^{\ast}
for at least one index
j
we have

\eta([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{\ell}}])=0.

To prove this, note that
Q_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}}=v_{i_{j}}
and
P_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}}=0
and using the last bullet point of Lemma
7.1
, we have

\eta([v_{i_{1}},\ldots,v_{i_{\ell}}])=\eta([P_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}},\ldots,Q_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}},\ldots,P_{i_{\ell}}v_{i_{\ell}}]),

similar to the argument in Step 3.

Next note that
P_{i}Q_{i}v=0
for all
v\in V_{i}
and therefore

P_{i}(L_{i})\leqslant P_{i}(\pi_{1}(\widetilde{K_{i}}\cap\{(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})\in V_{i}^{\otimes 4}\colon Q_{i}v_{2}=Q_{i}v_{3}=Q_{i}v_{4}=0\})).

Therefore we may lift
P_{i_{\ell}}v_{i_{\ell}}
for
\ell\neq j
to
\widetilde{v_{i_{\ell}}}=(P_{i_{\ell}}v_{i_{\ell}}+w_{i_{\ell}},P_{i_{\ell}}v_{i_{\ell}},P_{i_{\ell}}v_{i_{\ell}},P_{i_{\ell}}v_{i_{\ell}})\in\widetilde{K_{i_{\ell}}}
where
w_{i_{\ell}}\in Q_{i_{\ell}}(V_{i_{\ell}})
. We lift
v_{i_{j}}
to
\widetilde{v_{i_{j}}}
which has the form
(Q_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}},0,0,0)\in\widetilde{K_{i_{j}}}
.

Note that we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathrm{Prod}}([\widetilde{v_{i_{1}}},\ldots,\widetilde{v_{i_{r}}}])=\eta([P_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}}+w_{i_{1}},\ldots,Q_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}},\ldots,P_{i_{r}}v_{i_{r}}+w_{i_{r}}])

\displaystyle=\eta([P_{i_{1}}v_{i_{1}},\ldots,Q_{i_{j}}v_{i_{j}},\ldots,P_{i_{r}}v_{i_{r}}])

where in the first equality we have used for all
\ell
that
\widetilde{v_{i_{\ell}}}\in\widetilde{K_{i_{\ell}}}
and the result from Step 3, in the second equality that
\widetilde{v_{i_{j}}}
has the final three coordinates identically zero, and in the final equality that
w_{i_{\ell}}\in Q_{i_{\ell}}(V_{i_{\ell}})=V_{i_{\ell},\mathrm{Dep}}
and the final item of Lemma
7.1
.

The desired decomposition of spaces for the lemma will have

\displaystyle W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}

\displaystyle:=L_{i}^{\ast},\quad W_{i,\ast}:=P_{i}(L_{i})\leqslant L_{i}\cap P_{i}(V_{i}).

The fact
P_{i}(L_{i})\leqslant L_{i}\cap P_{i}(V_{i})
is deduced from
Q_{i}(V_{i})\leqslant L_{i}
.
W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}
will be constructed explicitly in the next step but is chosen so that

W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}\leqslant Q_{i}(L_{i})=Q_{i}(V_{i})=V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}

and
W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}=V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}}\leqslant L_{i}
. Given these properties of
W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}
, note that the above analysis, along with Lemma
7.1
, establishes the final bullet point for our output.

Step 5: Controlling approximate homomorphisms.

Recall
\widetilde{K_{i}}\leqslant S_{i}
and there is a natural isomorphism of groups

S_{i}\simeq\{(v,v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})\colon v\in P_{i}(V_{i}),v_{1},\ldots,v_{4}\in Q_{i}(V_{i})\}.

Using this as an identification, we may write

\widetilde{K_{i}}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{\dim(S_{i})-\dim(\widetilde{K_{i}})}\operatorname{ker}((\xi_{j}^{P_{i}},\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}},\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}},-\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}},-\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}))

where
\xi_{j}^{P_{i}}\in P_{i}(V_{i})^{\vee}
and
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}\in Q_{i}(V_{i})^{\vee}
(i.e., corresponding dual vector spaces). Note that the annihilators all have the special form of
(\cdot,\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}},\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}},-\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}},-\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}})
since

\{(Q_{i}v_{1},Q_{i}v_{2},Q_{i}v_{3},Q_{i}v_{4})\in V_{i}^{\otimes 4}\colon Q_{i}v_{1}+Q_{i}v_{2}-Q_{i}v_{3}-Q_{i}v_{4}=0\}=R_{i}\leqslant\widetilde{K_{i}}.

Note that

L_{i}^{\ast}=\{v\in V_{i}\colon P_{i}v=0\text{ and }\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}(Q_{i}v)=0\text{ for all }j\}

since
v\in L_{i}^{\ast}
is equivalent under this identification to
(0,Q_{i}v,0,0,0)\in\widetilde{K_{i}}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that for
1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{i}^{\ast})
, vectors
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}
are independent in
Q_{i}(V_{i})^{\vee}
(and they must span the orthogonal space to
L_{i}^{\ast}
within
Q_{i}(V_{i})^{\vee}
).

By appropriate scaling, we may assume
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}(w_{i,j})
is an integer bounded by
M(\delta)
for
1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})
. We extend each
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}
to an operator on
(G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)})^{\vee}
by setting
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}(w_{i,j})=0
for
j>\dim(V_{i,\mathrm{Dep}})
. Possibly at the cost of another
M(\delta)
scaling, we may assume that
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}(\Gamma\cap G_{(i,1)}~\mathrm{mod}~G_{(i,2)})\in\mathbb{Z}
. We extend
\xi_{j}^{P_{i}}(\cdot)
in an analogous manner to
(G_{(i,1)}/G_{(i,2)})^{\vee}
by setting
\xi_{j}^{P_{i}}(w_{i,j})=0
for
1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{i},\mathrm{Dep})
and
j>\dim(V_{i})
. Again, we may scale such that
\xi_{j}^{P_{i}}(\Gamma\cap G_{(i,1)}~\mathrm{mod}~G_{(i,2)})\in\mathbb{Z}
. The crucial point here is that now
\xi_{j}^{P_{i}}
and
\xi_{j}^{Q_{i}}
are
i
-th horizontal characters of height at most
M(\delta)
.

At this point, we now let the parameter
\vec{i}
be a vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{D^{\prime}}
. We have

\tau_{|\vec{i}|}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{\vec{h}}))\in K_{|\vec{i}|}

and thus

\operatorname{dist}(\tau_{|\vec{i}|}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast})),S_{|\vec{i}|}+T^{-1}\operatorname{Horiz}_{|\vec{i}|}(\Gamma^{\otimes 4}))\leq M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|}

after Pigeonholing
\vec{h}
appropriately. Here distance is in
L^{\infty}
after expressing both of these expressions in the basis
\exp(\mathcal{X}_{|\vec{i}|})^{\otimes 4}
and
T
is an integer bounded by
M(\delta)
.

Furthermore note that

\tau_{|\vec{i}|}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}))\in S_{|\vec{i}|}

by Lemma
7.1
. So if we choose a set of horizontal characters of height
M(\delta)
relative to
\operatorname{Horiz}_{|\vec{i}|}(\Gamma^{\otimes 4})
which cut out
\widetilde{K_{|\vec{i}|}}
as their common kernel, then noting that
K_{|\vec{i}|}\cap S_{|\vec{i}|}\leqslant\widetilde{K_{|\vec{i}|}}
and applying Lemma
A.5
, we may assume that

(7.1)

\tau_{|\vec{i}|}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{\vec{h}}))\in\widetilde{K_{|\vec{i}|}}

and
\varepsilon_{\vec{h}},\gamma_{\vec{h}}
have identical properties up to changing implicit constants. We will assume this refined property of the factorization for the remainder of our analysis.

Given the factorization of
g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}
, we thus deduce (taking an appropriate least common multiple) that

\displaystyle\bigg\lVert T_{1}\cdot\bigg(\xi_{j}^{P_{|\vec{i}|}}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{2}}))+\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}}))+\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{2}}))

(7.2)

\displaystyle\quad-\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{3}}))-\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{4}}))\bigg)\bigg\rVert_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|}

for all
1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})
where
T_{1}
is an integer bounded by
M(\delta)
. Here we have used that
\xi_{j}^{P_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}))
is equal for all
h\in H
by Lemma
7.1
.

We define functions
f,g\colon H\to\mathbb{R}^{\sum_{|\vec{i}|<d-1}\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})}
via

\displaystyle f(h)

\displaystyle=(T_{1}\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h})))_{|\vec{i}|<d-1,1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})},

\displaystyle g(h)

\displaystyle=(T_{1}\xi_{j}^{P_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}))+T_{1}\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h})))_{1\leq|\vec{i}|\leq d-1,~1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})}.

Note that for the additive quadruples on which we have (
7.2
), we are exactly in the situation necessary to apply results on approximate homomorphisms.

In particular, we may apply Lemma
B.2
. We see that there exists
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
having density at least
\epsilon(\delta)
such that for all
\vec{i},j
and
h\in H^{\prime}
, we have

(7.3)

\bigg\lVert T_{1}\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h}))-\bigg(\gamma_{\vec{i},j}+\sum_{k=1}^{d^{\ast}}\alpha_{\vec{i},j,k}\{\beta_{k}h\}\bigg)\bigg\rVert_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|},

where:

•

d^{\ast}\leq m(\delta)
;

•

\beta_{k}\in(1/N^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}
where
N^{\prime}
is a prime between
100N
and
200N
.

At this point, for each
\vec{i}
we find elements
Z_{|\vec{i}|,j}
for
1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})
which are
M(\delta)
-rational combinations of
\{w_{|\vec{i}|,j}\colon 1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})\}
such that

(7.4)

T_{1}\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(Z_{|\vec{i}|,j})=1\text{ and }\xi_{j^{\prime}}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(Z_{|\vec{i}|,j})=0

for
j^{\prime}\neq j
such that
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})
. We define

W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}((Z_{|\vec{i}|,j})_{1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})}).

We see that there are no nontrivial linear relations between
W_{|\vec{i}|,\ast}
and
W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}
since
W_{|\vec{i}|,\ast}\leqslant P_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|})
and
W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}\leqslant Q_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|})
. There are no linear relations between
W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}
and
W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}
as
W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}
lies in the joint kernel of the
\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}
and therefore using (
7.4
) one can prove any such relation is trivial. Furthermore, by construction we have
V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}}=Q_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|})=W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}
. Finally
L_{|\vec{i}|}=P_{|\vec{i}|}(L_{|\vec{i}|})+Q_{|\vec{i}|}(L_{|\vec{i}|})=W_{|\vec{i}|,\ast}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}
; this implicitly uses
Q(L_{|\vec{i}|})=Q_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|})\leqslant L_{|\vec{i}|}
.

Step 6: Constructing the desired factorizations and completing the proof.

Using the refined factorization (
7.1
) implies that

\pi_{1}(\tau(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{\vec{h}})))\in L_{|\vec{i}|}

since
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{K_{|\vec{i}|}})\leqslant L_{|\vec{i}|}
. Applying
g_{\vec{h}}^{\ast}=\varepsilon_{\vec{h}}\cdot\widetilde{g_{\vec{h}}}\cdot\gamma_{\vec{h}}
in the first coordinate then implies that

(7.5)

\operatorname{dist}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}}),L_{|\vec{i}|}+T_{2}^{-1}\operatorname{Horiz}_{i}(\Gamma))\leq M(\delta)N^{-|\vec{i}|}

for
h_{1}\in H^{\prime}
where distance is in
L^{\infty}
after expressing values in terms of
\exp(\mathcal{X}_{i})
. Here
T_{2}
is an integer bounded by
M(\delta)
. Furthermore recall from Lemma
7.1
that

(7.6)

\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h})-\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h^{\prime}})\in V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}}=Q_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|})

for
h,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\subseteq H
.

Let
Y_{|\vec{i}|,j}\in\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{X}\cap\log(G_{(|\vec{i}|,1)})\setminus\mathcal{X}\cap\log(G_{(|\vec{i}|,2)}))
be such that
\exp(Y_{|\vec{i}|,j})~\mathrm{mod}~G_{(|\vec{i}|,2)}=Z_{|\vec{i}|,j}
. Then for
h\in H^{\prime}
, we define (with the product taken in, say, lexicographic order)

(7.7)

\widetilde{g}_{h}(n)=\prod_{|\vec{i}|\leq d}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(Y_{|\vec{i}|,j})^{T_{1}^{-1}\binom{n}{\vec{i}}\cdot(\gamma_{\vec{i},j}+\sum_{k=1}^{d^{\ast}}\alpha_{\vec{i},j,k}\{\beta_{k}h\})}.

By construction and Lemma
2.12
, for
h,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{h}^{-1}g_{h})-\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{h^{\prime}}^{-1}g_{h^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\in Q_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{|\vec{i}|}(\widetilde{g}_{h}^{-1}g_{h}),L_{|\vec{i}|}+T_{2}^{-1}\operatorname{Horiz}_{|\vec{i}|}(\Gamma))

\displaystyle\leq M(\delta)\cdot N^{-|\vec{i}|},

\displaystyle\lVert T_{1}\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}(\widetilde{g}_{h}^{-1}g_{h})\rVert_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle\leq M(\delta)\cdot N^{-|\vec{i}|},

where
1\leq|\vec{i}|\leq d-1
and
1\leq j\leq\dim(V_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Dep}})-\dim(L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast})
. The first line comes from (
7.6
), the second line from (
7.5
), and the third from (
7.3
) and (
7.7
), in conjunction with (
7.4
).

We now fix an element
h_{2}\in H^{\prime}
. For each
h_{1}\in H^{\prime}
we write

\displaystyle g_{h_{1}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{g}_{h_{1}}\cdot(\widetilde{g}_{h_{1}}^{-1}g_{h_{1}}^{\prime})=\widetilde{g}_{h_{1}}\cdot(\widetilde{g}_{h_{1}}^{-1}g_{h_{1}}^{\prime})\cdot(\widetilde{g}_{h_{2}}^{-1}g_{h_{2}}^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot(\widetilde{g}_{h_{2}}^{-1}g_{h_{2}}^{\prime}).

By applying Lemma
A.5
, we may write

(\widetilde{g}_{h_{1}}^{-1}g_{h_{1}}^{\prime})\cdot(\widetilde{g}_{h_{2}}^{-1}g_{h_{2}}^{\prime})^{-1}=\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}g_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast},\qquad(\widetilde{g}_{h_{2}}^{-1}g_{h_{2}}^{\prime})=\varepsilon^{\ast}g^{\ast}\gamma^{\ast}

where
\gamma^{\ast},\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast}
are
M(\delta)
-rational,
\varepsilon^{\ast},\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}
are
(M(\delta),\vec{N})
-smooth, and we have
\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{1}}^{\ast})\in L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast}=W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}
using the first and third lines above and
\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g^{\ast})\in L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast}+P_{|\vec{i}|}(L_{|\vec{i}|})=W_{|\vec{i}|,\ast}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}
using the second and third lines above. (Recall that
L_{|\vec{i}|}^{\ast}\leqslant Q_{|\vec{i}|}(V_{|\vec{i}|})
is cut out by the
\xi_{j}^{Q_{|\vec{i}|}}
.) Additionally, these sequences are the identity at
0
.

Therefore, for
h_{1}\in H^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle g_{h_{1}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast}((\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast})^{-1}\widetilde{g}_{h_{1}}(\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast}))((\varepsilon^{\ast})^{-1}g_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast})((\varepsilon^{\ast})^{-1}\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast}(\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast})^{-1})(\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast}g^{\ast}(\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast})^{-1})(\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\gamma^{\ast})

\displaystyle=:(\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast})\cdot g_{h_{1}}^{\triangle}\cdot(\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\gamma^{\ast}).

So, for
h_{3},h_{4}\in H
we deduce using Lemma
2.12
and the above analysis that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{h_{3}})

\displaystyle\in W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{3}}^{\triangle})

\displaystyle\in L_{|\vec{i}|}=W_{|\vec{i}|,\ast}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}}+W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Proj}_{W_{|\vec{i}|},\mathrm{Lin}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h_{3}}^{\triangle}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Proj}_{W_{|\vec{i}|},\mathrm{Lin}}(\operatorname{Taylor}_{|\vec{i}|}(\widetilde{g}_{h_{3}})),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{h_{3}}^{-1}g_{h_{3}}^{\triangle})-\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(\widetilde{g}_{h_{4}}^{-1}g_{h_{4}}^{\triangle})

\displaystyle\in W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}}.

Furthermore note that
\varepsilon_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\varepsilon^{\ast}
is sufficiently smooth and
\gamma_{h_{1}}^{\ast}\gamma^{\ast}
is appropriately rational. This proves the lemma provided we handle the exceptional case of
d=2
which we will next step.

Step 7: Handling the exceptional case
d=2
.

In this exceptional case, we have
r=1
and
d=2
, and
\eta
is nontrivial. In this case, we have
\psi_{h}\equiv 1
, by projecting to the one-dimensional subgroup generated by
\eta
, we may assume that the nilmanifold underlying
\chi_{h}
is one-dimensional and
\chi_{h}=e(\vec{\xi_{h}}\cdot)
. By pigeonholing in a progression and applying the hypothesis, we have that for some integer
T\leq M(\delta)
, and a proportion
\epsilon(\delta)
of additive quadruples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
in
H
that for each component
i

\|T(\xi_{h_{1}}^{i}+\xi_{h_{2}}^{i}-\xi_{h_{3}}^{i}-\xi_{h_{4}}^{i})\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}}\leq M(\delta)N^{-1}.

Applying Lemma
B.2
, there exists a subset
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
of size at least
\epsilon(\delta)|H|
such that

\xi_{h}^{i}=\gamma+\sum_{k=1}^{d^{*}}\alpha_{i,k}\{\beta_{i,k}\cdot h\}+M(\delta)^{-1}\mathbb{Z}

where

•

d^{\ast}\leq m(\delta)
;

•

\beta_{i,k}\in(1/N^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}
where
N^{\prime}
is a prime between
100N
and
200N
.

We can then take
W_{1,\mathrm{Lin}}
to be the one-dimensional subspace generated by
\eta
, and
W_{1,*}
the orthogonal complement, and
W_{1,\mathrm{Pet}}
to be the zero subspace. Each of these spaces enjoy
M(\delta)
-rational bases via
[
34
, Lemma B.11]
and dividing
\gamma
by
\alpha_{i,k}
by an integer bounded by
M(\delta)
, we have achieved the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

7.3.
Constructing a higher step nilsequence from bracket linear coefficients

We are now ready to prove Proposition
5.2
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

The proof is again very similar to that of
[
38
, Lemma 6.3]
, but as we work with multidimensional (input) nilsequences, we must make some modifications. Unfortunately, the nature of the modifications we make are such that they require extensive justification; hence, our proof is still rather lengthy.

We begin with the following lemma which is analogous to
[
38
, Lemma 10.2]
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold with filtration degree
k
and consider the adapted Malcev basis
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\}
. Let
g:\mathbb{Z}^{D}\to G
be a polynomial sequence. Then
g
admits a representation of the form

g(\vec{n})=\prod_{i=0}^{k}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=m-\mathrm{dim}(G_{i})+1}^{m}\exp(X_{j})^{\alpha_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

(with the product along
x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i
taken in any order with the coefficients
\alpha_{x,j}
depending on the order in which we take the product).

Proof.

By Baker-Campbell Hausdorff and Taylor expansion, we may write

g(n)=\exp\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sum_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}g_{x}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}\right)

with
g_{x}\in\log(G_{|x|})
. We now take
g_{0}(n):=g(n)
,
g_{0,j}:=g_{j}
, and iteratively define a sequence
g_{\ell}(n)
by the following process: for
|x|=\ell
, write
g_{x,\ell}=\sum_{j=m-\mathrm{dim}(G_{\ell})+1}\alpha_{x,j}X_{j}
. Then let (for the outer product in any order)

g_{\ell+1}(n):=\left(\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=\ell}\prod_{j=m-\mathrm{dim}(G_{\ell})+1}^{m}\exp(X_{j})^{\alpha_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}\right)^{-1}g_{\ell}(n).

By Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff, we may write for some
g_{x,\ell+1}

g_{\ell+1}(n)=\exp\left(\sum_{i=\ell+1}^{k}\sum_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}g_{x,\ell+1}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}\right)

from which we complete the inductive definition of
g_{\ell}
. Note that
\alpha_{x,j}
exists and is well-defined since
\mathcal{X}
is a nesting basis. In addition,
g_{x,\ell+1}
exists and is well-defined since inductively and the nesting property we see that
g_{\ell+1}
has no Taylor coefficients of degree
0
to
\ell
. This completes the proof.
∎

We will now indicate modifications one must make for the proof of Proposition
5.2
. Let
H^{*}
denote the set of
h
’s from the linearization step. Again, we deal with multidimensional input nilcharacters rather than single dimensional input nilcharacters. We must take care that the Taylor expansions we work with are multidimensional. Thus, our Taylor coefficients are slightly different. Instead of
[
38
, Page 52 bullet point 8]
, we work with

\displaystyle\operatorname{Taylor}_{\vec{i}}(g_{h})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\ast})}\exp(Z_{|\vec{i}|,j}^{\ast})^{z_{x,j}^{\ast}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Pet}})}\exp(Z_{|\vec{i}|,j}^{\mathrm{Pet}})^{z_{\vec{i},j}^{h,\mathrm{Pet}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{|\vec{i}|,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(Z_{|\vec{i}|,j}^{\mathrm{Lin}})^{z_{\vec{i},j}^{h,\mathrm{Lin}}}~\mathrm{mod}~G_{(|\vec{i}|,2)}

where

z_{\vec{i},j}^{h,\mathrm{Lin}}=\gamma_{\vec{i},j}+\sum_{k=1}^{d^{\ast}}\alpha_{\vec{i},j,k}\{\beta_{k}\cdot h\}

where
d^{\ast}\leq m(\delta)
and
\beta_{k}\in(1/N^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}^{D}
where
N^{\prime}
is a prime in
[100N,200N]
.

Also, we work with the following expansions of
g_{h}^{*}
,
g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin}}
, and
g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet}}
.

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\ast})}\exp(z_{x,j}^{\ast})^{z_{x,j}^{\ast}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}\cdot\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(Z_{i,j}^{\mathrm{Lin}})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}},

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(Z_{i,j}^{\mathrm{Lin}})^{(z_{x,j}^{h,\mathrm{Lin}}-\gamma_{x,j})\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}},

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}})}\exp(Z_{i,j}^{\mathrm{Pet}})^{z_{x,j}^{h,\mathrm{Pet}}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

We similarly define
g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem}}
via
g_{h}=g_{h}^{*}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin}}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet}}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem}}
. Thus, by Lemma
7.4
, we have

g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem}}=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=\dim(G)-\dim(G_{i,1})+1}^{\dim(G)}\exp(X_{j})^{\kappa_{x,j}^{h}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}.

We now lift to the universal nilmanifold. We require the following definition, which is
[
38
, Defintion 10.3]
.

Definition 7.5
.

The
universal nilmanifold of degree-rank
(s-1,r^{\ast})
and the associated discrete cocompact subgroup are defined as follows. We write
G_{\mathrm{Univ}}=G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\vec{D}}
where
\vec{D}=\vec{D}^{\ast}+\vec{D}^{\mathrm{Lin}}+\vec{D}^{\mathrm{Pet}}
with
\vec{D}^{\ast},\vec{D}^{\mathrm{Lin}},\vec{D}^{\mathrm{Pet}}\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{s-1}
. We specify
G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\vec{D}}
by formal generators of the Lie algebra

e_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq s-1
and
1\leq j\leq D_{i}
where
D_{i}=D_{i}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Pet}}
with the relations:

•

Any
(r-1)
-fold commutator of
e_{i_{1},j_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{r},j_{r}}
with
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}>(s-1)
vanishes;

•

Any
(r-1)
-fold commutator of
e_{i_{1},j_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{r},j_{r}}
with
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=(s-1)
and
r>r^{\ast}
vanishes.

The associated discrete group which we will be concerned with is
\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}}
which is the discrete group generated by
\exp(e_{i,j})
for
1\leq i\leq s-1
and
1\leq j\leq D_{i}
.

Also, when we lift to the universal nilmanifold, we will define

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{Univ}}(n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\ast})}\exp(e_{i,j})^{z_{x,j}^{\ast}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(e_{i,j+\dim(W_{i,\ast})})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{\alpha}}{x!}},

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin},\mathrm{Univ}}(n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\prod_{k=1}^{d^{\ast}}\exp(e_{i,D_{i}^{\ast}+(j-1)d^{\ast}+k})^{\alpha_{x,j,k}\{\beta_{k}\cdot h\}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}},

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet},\mathrm{Univ}}(n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}})}\exp(e_{i,j+D_{i}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}})^{z_{x,j}^{h,\mathrm{Pet}}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}},

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem},\mathrm{Univ}}(n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(G_{(i,2)})}\exp(e_{i,j+D_{i}-\dim(G_{(i,2)})})^{\kappa_{i,j}^{h}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

and

g_{h}^{\mathrm{Univ}}:=g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{Univ}}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin},\mathrm{Univ}}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet},\mathrm{Univ}}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem},\mathrm{Univ}}.

We define a homomorphism
\phi\colon G_{\mathrm{Univ}}\to G
by defining the map on generators. Define

\displaystyle\phi(\exp(e_{i,j}))=\begin{cases}\exp(z_{x,j}^{\ast})&\text{ if }1\leq j\leq\dim(W_{i,\ast}),\\
\exp(Z_{i,j-\dim(W_{i,\ast})}^{\mathrm{Lin}})&\text{ if }\dim(W_{i,\ast})+1\leq j\leq\dim(W_{i,\ast})+\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})=D_{i}^{\ast},\\
\exp(Z_{i,\ell}^{\mathrm{Lin}})&\text{ if }1+(\ell-1)d^{\ast}\leq j-D_{i}^{\ast}\leq\ell d^{\ast}\text{ for }1\leq\ell\leq\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}}),\\
\exp(Z_{i,j-D_{i}^{\ast}-D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}}^{\mathrm{Pet}})&\text{ if }D_{i}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}+1\leq j\leq D_{i}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}+\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Pet}}),\\
\exp(X_{j-D_{i}+\dim(G)})&\text{ if }D_{i}-\dim(G_{(i,2)})+1\leq j\leq D_{i}.\end{cases}

We now define

\widetilde{F}(g\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}}):=F(\phi(g)\Gamma).

Similar to
[
38
, Claim 10.6]
, we have the following. (Note that
\exp(e_{i,j})
often does not get sent to
Z_{x,j}
.)

Claim 7.6
.

Given the above setup, we have

\widetilde{F}(g_{h}^{\mathrm{Univ}}(n)\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}})=F(g_{h}(n)\Gamma)=\chi_{h}(n).

We next define the quotient universal nilmanifold in which we lift to.

Definition 7.7
.

We define
G_{\mathrm{Rel}}=G_{\mathrm{Rel}}^{\vec{D}^{\ast},\vec{D}^{\mathrm{Lin}},\vec{D}^{\mathrm{Pet}}}
as the Lie subgroup of
G_{\mathrm{Univ}}
where
\log(G_{\mathrm{Rel}})
is spanned by:

•

Any
(r-1)
-fold commutator of
e_{i_{1},j_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{r},j_{r}}
with at least one index
\ell
such that
j_{\ell}>D^{\ast}_{i_{\ell}}+D^{\mathrm{Lin}}_{i_{\ell}}
;

•

Any
(r-1)
-fold commutator of
e_{i_{1},j_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{r},j_{r}}
with
j_{\ell}>D^{\ast}_{i_{\ell}}
for at least two distinct indices
\ell
.

We then define
G_{\mathrm{Quot}}
as
G_{\mathrm{Quot}}:=G_{\mathrm{Univ}}/G_{\mathrm{Rel}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{Quot}}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}}/(\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}}\cap G_{\mathrm{Rel}})
.

As in
[
38
, Lemma 10.10, Lemma 10.11]
, we have the following.

Lemma 7.8
.

Given the above setup, let
G_{\mathrm{Quot}}=G_{\mathrm{Quot}}^{\vec{D}}
and note that
G_{\mathrm{Quot}}
has a degree-rank
(d-1,r)
filtration given by

(G_{\mathrm{Quot}})_{(d^{\prime},r^{\prime})}=(G_{\mathrm{Univ}})_{(d^{\prime},r^{\prime})}/((G_{\mathrm{Univ}})_{(d^{\prime},r^{\prime})}\cap G_{\mathrm{Rel}}).

Furthermore the dimension of
G_{\mathrm{Univ}}
is bounded by
O_{s}(\lVert D\rVert_{\infty}^{O_{K}(1)})
and one may find an adapted Mal’cev basis
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Quot}}
such that the complexity of
G_{\mathrm{Quot}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{Quot}}
is
\exp(\lVert D\rVert_{\infty}^{O_{K}(1)})
.

We will define

g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{Quot}}(n)=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\ast})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j})^{z_{x,j}^{\ast}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j+\dim(W_{i,\ast})})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin},\mathrm{Quot}}(n)=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\prod_{k=1}^{d^{\ast}}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,D_{i}^{\ast}+(j-1)d^{\ast}+k})^{\alpha_{x,j,k}\{\beta_{k}\cdot h\}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

Note that

g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{Quot}}=g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{Univ}}~\mathrm{mod}~G_{\mathrm{Rel}},\qquad g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin},\mathrm{Quot}}=g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin},\mathrm{Univ}}~\mathrm{mod}~G_{\mathrm{Rel}}.

We will define

g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet},\mathrm{Quot}}=g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem},\mathrm{Quot}}=\mathrm{Id},g_{h}^{\mathrm{Quot}}=g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{Quot}}\cdot g_{h}^{\mathrm{Lin},\mathrm{Quot}}.

Given this setup, we have the following which is exactly
[
38
, Lemma 10.12]
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Given the above setup, let

G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle}:=\{(g,g~\mathrm{mod}~G_{\mathrm{Rel}})\in G_{\mathrm{Univ}}\times G_{\mathrm{Quot}}\colon g\in G_{\mathrm{Univ}}\}

which is given the degree-rank filtration

(G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle})_{(d,r)}:=\{(g,g~\mathrm{mod}~G_{\mathrm{Rel}})\in(G_{\mathrm{Univ}})_{(d,r)}\times(G_{\mathrm{Quot}})_{(d,r)}\colon g\in(G_{\mathrm{Univ}})_{(d,r)}\}.

Define

\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle}=G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle}\cap(\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}}\times\Gamma_{\mathrm{Quot}})
. We have:

•

(g_{h}^{\mathrm{Univ}},g_{h}^{\mathrm{Quot}})
is a polynomial sequence on
G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle}
with respect to the given degree-rank filtration;

•

The function

(g,g^{\prime})\mapsto\widetilde{F}(g\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}})\otimes\overline{F^{\ast}}(g^{\prime}\Gamma_{\mathrm{Quot}})

for
(g,g^{\prime})\in G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle}
is
(G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle})_{(d-1,r)}
-invariant;

•

G_{\mathrm{Univ}}^{\triangle}
has complexity bounded by
M(\delta)
;

•

Each coordinate of
\widetilde{F}(g\Gamma_{\mathrm{Univ}})\otimes\overline{F^{\ast}}(g^{\prime}\Gamma_{\mathrm{Quot}})
is
M(\delta)
-Lipschitz.

The point, of course, is the second bullet point, which states that the
F(g_{h}(n)\Gamma)
and
\widetilde{F}(g_{h}^{\mathrm{Univ}}\Gamma)
differ by a nilsequence of strictly lower degree-rank via viewing
F\otimes\overline{\widetilde{F}}
on a quotient nilmanifold of the product.

We will now actually construct the additional nilmanifold which incorporates the bracket linear structure in the nilmanifold formalism. Via reindexing, we may write

g_{h}^{\mathrm{Quot}}(n)=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j+\dim(W_{i,\ast})})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j})^{\alpha_{x,j}\{\beta_{x,j}\cdot h\}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}.

The next part of the proof will contain the most significant changes from
[
38
]
.
Since
\{\beta_{x,j}\cdot h\}
are multidimensional in
x
, we will need to lift to a larger universal nilmanifold. Thankfully, since our undesirable
h
-dependent terms
g_{h}^{\mathrm{Pet}}
and
g_{h}^{\mathrm{Rem}}
are eliminated from the argument above, this makes it so that we do not need to run a multidegree-rank induction argument. We now define the multidegree universal nilmanifold with associated dimensional data. We now consider
G_{\mathrm{Multi-Univ}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi-Univ}}
to be the same as
G_{\mathrm{Univ}}
except for each
x
, we have generators
e_{x,j}
with
e_{x,j}
satisfying the same commutator relations with
e_{|x|,j}
. Similarly, we define
G_{\mathrm{MultiRel}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiRel}}
as the same as
G_{\mathrm{Rel}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{Rel}}
except that we take generators
e_{x,j}
instead of
e_{i,j}
and relations with
e_{x,j}
satisfying the same commutator relations with
\widetilde{e}_{|x|,j}
. Take
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
as
G_{\mathrm{Multi-Univ}}/G_{\mathrm{MultiRel}}
and specify
\widetilde{e}_{x,j}
as projection of
e_{x,j}
. We define
\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
similarly.

We now lift
F^{*}
to
F_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}^{*}
on
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
by taking each generator
\widetilde{e}_{x,j}
to
\widetilde{e}_{|x|,j}
. Hence, defining

g_{h}^{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}(n)=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{x,j+\dim(W_{i,\ast})})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{x,j})^{\alpha_{x,j}\{\beta_{x,j}\cdot h\}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}},

we see that
F_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}^{*}(g_{h}^{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}(n)\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})
is a well-defined nilsequence on
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
.

We now define
G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
to be the Lie subgroup of
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
such that
\log(G_{\mathrm{Lin}})
is the subspace generated by all
(r-1)
-fold iterated commutators (with
r\geq 1
) of
\widetilde{e}_{x_{1},j_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{e}_{x_{r},j_{r}}
with
j_{\ell}>D_{|x|_{\ell}}^{\ast}
for exactly one index
\ell
. We have the following observation, which is essentially
[
38
, Claim 11.1]
.

Claim 7.10
.

We have that
G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
is well-defined, abelian, and normal with respect to
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
.

Due to normality,
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
acts on
G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
via conjugation. In particular, we define
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}\rtimes G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
with the group law given by

(g,g_{1})(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}):=(gg^{\prime},g_{1}^{g^{\prime}}g_{1}^{\prime})=(gg^{\prime},((g^{\prime})^{-1}g_{1}g^{\prime})g_{1}^{\prime}).

We now introduce a manner in which the additive group
R=\mathbb{R}^{\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\sum_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}D_{x}^{\mathrm{Lin}}}
, with elements denoted

t=(t_{x,j})_{x,~D_{|x|,\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}},

acts on
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}\ltimes G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
.

For each
t\in R
, we define the homomorphism
g\mapsto g^{t}
from
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
to itself on generators. We map
\exp(\widetilde{e}_{x,j})\to\exp(\widetilde{e}_{x,j})^{t_{x,j}}
for
1\leq|x|\leq d-1
and
D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}
while
\exp(\widetilde{e}_{x,j})
is fixed
for
1\leq|x|\leq d-1
and
1\leq j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}
and we define

\rho\colon R\to\operatorname{Aut}(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}\rtimes G_{\mathrm{Lin}})
by

\rho(t)(g,g_{1}):=(g\cdot g_{1}^{t},g_{1}).

Via calculations in
[
38
, pg. 60]
,
\rho
is well-defined. Thus, we can take the semidirect product
G_{\mathrm{Multi}}=R\rtimes_{\rho}(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}\rtimes G_{\mathrm{Lin}})
with respect to
\rho
. We give
G_{\mathrm{Multi}}
a multi-degree filtration as follows. For
d_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
and
d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}^{D}
, we define

•

If
x_{1}>1
then
(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(x_{1},d_{2})}=\mathrm{Id}_{G_{\mathrm{Multi}}}
;

•

If
|d_{2}|>0
then
(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,d_{2})}=\{(0,(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\colon g\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{d_{2}}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}
;

•

(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,0)}=\{(t,(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\colon t\in R,g\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{0}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}
or equivalently just
\{(t,(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\colon t\in R,g\in G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}
;

•

If
|d_{2}|>0
then
(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,d_{2})}=\{(0,(g,g_{1}))\colon g\in(G_{\mathrm{Quot}})_{d_{2}},g_{1}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{(d_{2},0)}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}
;

•

(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,0)}=G_{\mathrm{Multi}}
.

Claim 7.11
.

(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(d_{1},d_{2})}
is a valid multidegree filtration on
G_{\mathrm{Multi}}
.

Proof.

(The proof is little more than simply changing
G_{\mathrm{Quot}}
to
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
in
[
38
, Claim 11.2]
.) Note that

(t,(g,g_{1}))=(t,(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(0,(g,g_{1}))

and therefore
(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,0)}=(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,0)}\vee\bigvee_{i=1}^{D}(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,e_{i})}
. We next check various commutator relations. First note that

[(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,0)},(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,0)})]=\mathrm{Id}_{G_{\mathrm{Multi}}}.

This follows because if
g,h\in G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
we have
gh=hg
hence

(t,(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(t^{\prime},(h,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})))=(t+t^{\prime},(gh,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))=(t^{\prime},(h,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})))\cdot(t,(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})).

Therefore it suffices to verify that

\displaystyle[(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,\vec{a})},(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,\vec{b})}]

\displaystyle\leqslant(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,\vec{a}+\vec{b})},

\displaystyle[(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,\vec{a})},(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,\vec{b})}]

\displaystyle\leqslant(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,\vec{a}+\vec{b})}.

We first tackle the first claim, in which we may reduce to the case
|\vec{a}|,|\vec{b}|>0
. We wish to show

[(g,g_{1}),(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime})]\in\{(h,h_{1})\colon h\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{a}+\vec{b}},h_{1}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{a}+\vec{b}}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}

if
g,g_{1}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{a}}
,
g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{b}}
, and
g_{1},g_{1}^{\prime}\in G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
. It suffices to prove
(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{a}+\vec{b}}
is normal in
(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{a}}
and
(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{b}}
and then check at the level of generators.

To check normality, we have

\displaystyle(g,g_{1})(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime})(g,g_{1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(g,g_{1})(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime})(g^{-1},gg_{1}^{-1}g^{-1})

\displaystyle=(gg^{\prime},(g^{\prime})^{-1}g_{1}g^{\prime}\cdot g_{1}^{\prime})(g^{-1},gg_{1}^{-1}g^{-1})

\displaystyle=(gg^{\prime}g^{-1},(g(g^{\prime})^{-1})g_{1}(g^{\prime}g^{-1})\cdot gg_{1}^{\prime}g^{-1}\cdot gg_{1}^{-1}g^{-1})

and the result follows noting that
G_{\mathrm{Lin}},(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{\vec{a}}
are normal in
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
for all
\vec{a}\neq 0
.

Since

(g,g_{1})=(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})\cdot(\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}},g_{1})

and it suffices to check the claim on generators, we may reduce to the case where exactly one of
g,g_{1}
and exactly one of
g_{1},g_{1}^{\prime}
are the identity. The result is clear when
g,g^{\prime}
are trivial and the case when
g_{1},g_{1}^{\prime}
are trivial follows from the fact that we have a valid filtration on
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
. In the remaining cases, we may assume by symmetry that
g_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}
and
g^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}}
. We have

(g^{-1},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},(g_{1}^{\prime})^{-1})(g,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},g_{1}^{\prime})=(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},g^{-1}(g_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}gg_{1}^{\prime})

and we see that the final coordinate satisfies
[g,g_{1}^{\prime}]\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{(\vec{a}+\vec{b},0)}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
. We have finished verifying the first claim.

Now note that
\{(h,\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})\colon h\in G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}
is a normal subgroup of
G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}\ltimes G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
, since
G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
is abelian. Thus, combining with the first claim gives the second claim, namely

[(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,\vec{a})},(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(0,\vec{b})})]\leqslant(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,\vec{a}+\vec{b})},

for
|\vec{a}|>0
.

The only nontrivial case left is
\vec{a}=0
and
|\vec{b}|>0
for the second claim. Furthermore, combining what we know, it suffices to check the case when
(t,(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))
is the element from
(G_{\mathrm{Multi}})_{(1,0)}
. Note however that

\displaystyle(t,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(0,(g,g_{1}))\cdot(-t,(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(0,(g,g_{1}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=(t,(g,g_{1}))\cdot(-t,(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(0,(g^{-1},gg_{1}^{-1}g^{-1}))=(0,(gg_{1}^{-t},g_{1}))\cdot(0,(g^{-1},gg_{1}^{-1}g^{-1}))

\displaystyle=(0,(gg_{1}^{-t}g^{-1},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})).

and the fact that if
g,g_{1}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{(\vec{b},0)}
and
g_{1}\in G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
then
gg_{1}^{-t}g^{-1}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{(\vec{b},0)}
. This follows because if
g_{1}\in(G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})_{(\vec{b},0)}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
then
g_{1}^{t}
is in the same group.
∎

Writing
t=(t_{x,j})_{1\leq|x|\leq d-1,~D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}}
, we define

\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}}=\{(t,(g,g_{1}))\colon t_{x,j}\in\mathbb{Z},g\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}},g_{1}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}\cap G_{\mathrm{Lin}}\}.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
1
-bounded,
1
-periodic function such that:

•

\phi(x)=1
if
|\{x\}|\leq 1/2-2\epsilon(\delta)
;

•

\phi(x)=0
if
|\{x\}|\geq 1/2-\epsilon(\delta)
;

•

\phi
is
O(1/\epsilon(\delta))
-Lipschitz.

Define
H^{\ast}\subseteq H
such that for all
1\leq|x|\leq d-1
and
D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}
we have
|\{\beta_{x,j}\cdot h\}|\geq 1/2-\epsilon(\delta)
. Using the fact that
\beta_{x,j}\in(1/N^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}^{D}
where
N^{\prime}
is a prime between
100N
and
200N
, we see that there are at most
O(\epsilon(\delta)\cdot N^{D^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}})
indices which do not satisfy the criterion and choosing our parameter
\epsilon(\delta)
sufficiently small while still preserving the property of being
\epsilon(\delta)
, we may assume that
H^{\ast}
is at least half the size of
H
.

Given
(t,(g,g_{1}))\in G_{\mathrm{Multi}}
, we may find
(t^{\prime},(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}))\in(t,(g,g_{1}))\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}}
such that
(t^{\prime})_{i,j}\in(-1/2,1/2]
for all
i,j
. Define

F_{\mathrm{Multi}}((t,(g,g_{1}))\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}})=F^{\ast}(g^{\prime}\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq d-1\\
D_{i}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{i}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}\end{subarray}}\phi(t_{i,j}^{\prime});

we check that this in fact gives a well-defined function on
G_{\mathrm{Multi}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}}
. Note that if
(t^{\prime},(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}))\in(t,(g,g_{1}))\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}}
and
t_{i,j}^{\prime}\in(-1/2,1/2]
then
t_{i,j}^{\prime}=\{t_{i,j}\}
and hence
t^{\prime}
is unique. Furthermore, note that

(t^{\prime},(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}))\cdot(0,(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime}))=(t^{\prime},(g^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime})\cdot(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime}))=(t^{\prime},(g^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime},(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}g_{1}^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}))

and trivially

F^{\ast}(g^{\prime}\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})=F^{\ast}(g^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}})

if
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}
. Now recall that

\displaystyle g_{h}^{\ast,\mathrm{MultiQuot}}(n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\ast})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j})^{z_{x,j}^{\ast}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{\dim(W_{i,\mathrm{Lin}})}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j+\dim(W_{i,\ast})})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}.

We set

\displaystyle g_{0}(n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=1}^{D_{i}^{\ast}}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j})^{\gamma_{x,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}},\quad g_{1}(n)=\prod_{i\in[d-1]}\prod_{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{D}=i}\prod_{j=D_{i}^{\ast}+1}^{D_{i}^{\ast}+D_{i}^{\mathrm{Lin}}}\exp(\widetilde{e}_{i,j})^{\alpha_{i,j}\cdot\frac{n^{x}}{x!}}

and define

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{Final}}(h,n)

\displaystyle=(0,(g_{0}(n),g_{1}(n)))\cdot((\beta_{x,j}\cdot h)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq|x|\leq d-1\\
D_{i}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}\end{subarray}},(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))

\displaystyle=(0,(g_{0}(n),\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(0,(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},g_{1}(n)))\cdot((\beta_{x,j}\cdot h)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq|x|\leq d-1\\
D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}\end{subarray}},(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}})).

The crucial point is that for all
h\in H
,

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{Final}}(h,n)\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}}

\displaystyle=(0,(g_{0}(n),g_{1}(n)))\cdot((\{\beta_{i,j}\cdot h\})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq|x|\leq d-1\\
D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}\end{subarray}},(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}}

\displaystyle=((\{\beta_{x,j}h\})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq|x|\leq d-1\\
D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}\end{subarray}},(\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}},\mathrm{id}_{G_{\mathrm{Lin}}}))\cdot(0,(g_{0,h}^{\ast}(n),g_{1}(n)))\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}},

writing

g_{0,h}^{\ast}(n)=g_{0}(n)(g_{1}(n))^{t(h)}

where
t(h)=(\{\beta_{x,j}\cdot h\})_{1\leq|x|\leq d-1,~D_{|x|}^{\ast}<j\leq D_{|x|}^{\ast}+D_{|x|}^{\mathrm{Lin}}}\in R
. This is precisely the desired sense, discussed earlier, in which we have used the group action to “raise” parts of
G_{\mathrm{Lin}}
to
h
-fractional powers.

Therefore, for all
h\in H^{\ast}
we have

(7.8)

F_{\mathrm{Multi}}(g_{\mathrm{Final}}(h,n)\Gamma_{\mathrm{Multi}})=F^{\ast}(g_{h}^{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}(n)\Gamma_{\mathrm{MultiQuot}}).

This, in combination with (the discussion after) Lemma
7.9
completes the proof of Proposition
5.2
.
∎

8.
Proof of the multidimensional inverse theorem

The purpose of this section is to deduce Theorem
1.12
assuming that the theorem holds for
(\ell,\ell^{\prime}-1)
. This will be done via a symmetry and integration argument as in
[
38
, Section 12]

8.1.
Setup for the symmetry and integration arguments

We begin this section with the following proposition which is essentially the analogue of
[
38
, Theorem 6.4]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1/2)
,
\ell,\ell^{\prime},k\leq K
be parameters. Assume Theorem
1.12
for
(\ell,\ell^{\prime}-1)
. Let
f\colon[N]^{k}\to\mathbb{C}
be a
1
-bounded function such that

\lVert f\rVert_{U([N]^{k},\dots,[N]^{k},e_{1}[N],\dots,e_{\ell}[N])}\geq\delta

with
\ell^{\prime}
copies of
[N]
.

Then the following data exists:

•

A subset
H\subseteq[N]^{k}
of size at least
\epsilon(\delta)N^{k}
;

•

A multi-degree nilcharacter
\chi(h,n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(1,\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
;

•

For all
h\in H
, there exists one-bounded functions
f_{1,h},\dots,f_{k,h}
with
f_{i,h}
not depending on the
i
th coordinate and
\psi_{h}\in\mathrm{Nil}^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}-2}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
such that

\mathbb{E}_{h\in[N]^{k}}\|\mathbb{E}_{n\in[N]^{k}}\Delta_{h}f(n)\otimes\overline{\chi(h,n)}\otimes\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}f_{j,h}(n)\cdot\psi_{h}(n)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

We shall deduce this via a degree-rank induction argument from the following proposition; this is essentially our analogue of
[
38
, Proposition 6.3]
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1/2)
and
\ell,\ell^{\prime},k\leq K
be parameters. Let
f\colon[N]^{k}\to\mathbb{C}
be a
1
-bounded function such that for each
h\in H
, a subset of
[N]^{k}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{h\in[N]^{k}}\|\mathbb{E}_{n\in[N]^{k}}\Delta_{h}f(n)\otimes\chi_{h,0}(n)\otimes\overline{\chi_{0}(h,n)}\otimes\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}f_{j,h,0}(n)\cdot\psi_{h}(n)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta)

for

•

a multi-degree nilcharacter
\chi_{0}(h,n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(1,\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
that is a product of
O_{K}(1)

\mathbb{Z}
-multidegree nilcharacters also in
\mathrm{Nil}^{(1,\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
;

•

a degree-rank
(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)
nilcharacter
\chi_{h,0}(n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
that is a product of
O_{K}(1)

\mathbb{Z}
-multidegree nilcharacters in
\mathrm{Nil}^{(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
;

•

a nilsequence
\psi_{h}(n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}-2}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
;

•

and one bounded functions
f_{1,h,0},\dots,f_{k,h,0}
with
f_{i,h,0}
not depending on the
i
th coordinate.

Then the following data exists:

•

A subset
H\subseteq H
of size at least
\epsilon(\delta)|H|
;

•

A multi-degree nilcharacter
\chi(h,n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(1,\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
;

•

A degree-rank
(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)
nilcharacter
\chi_{h}(n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
;

•

For all
h\in H
, there exists one-bounded functions
f_{1,h},\dots,f_{\ell,h}
such that there exists
\psi_{h}\in\mathrm{Nil}^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}-2}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
such that

\mathbb{E}_{h\in[N]^{k}}\|\mathbb{E}_{n\in[N]^{k}}\Delta_{h}f(n)\otimes\chi_{h}(n)\otimes\overline{\chi(h,n)}\otimes\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}f_{j,h}(n)\|_{\infty}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

Proof.

First, we absorb the terms in the product of the
\mathbb{Z}
-multidegree nilcharacters which are degree
0
in the
e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}
variables inside the
f_{1,h},\dots,f_{\ell,h}
functions. By Lemma
6.2
, Proposition
5.2
, and the fact that
\chi_{h,0}(n)
and
\chi_{h,0}(n+n_{0})
are
(m(\delta),M(\delta))
equivalent for fixed
n_{0}
, we see that there exist nilcharacters
\widetilde{\chi_{h}}(n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{<(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
and
\widetilde{\chi}(h,n)\in\mathrm{Nil}^{(1,\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1)}(M(\delta),m(\delta),k,M(\delta))
such that
\chi_{h,0}
and
\widetilde{\chi}_{h}\otimes\widetilde{\chi}
are
(m(\delta),M(\delta))
-equivalent. The proposition then follows from Lemma
2.10
applied to these two nilcharacters.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

By the discussion in the beginning of Section 6, we see that there exists a family of nilcharacters
\chi_{h}\in\mathrm{Nil}^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1}(M(\delta),d(\delta),\ell,M(\delta))
and functions
f_{1,h},\dots,f_{\ell,h}
such that
f_{i,h}
does not depend on coordinate
i
, and

\mathbb{E}_{h\in[N]^{k}}\bigg\|\mathbb{E}_{n\in[N]^{k}}\Delta_{h}f(n)\chi_{h}(n)\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}f_{i,h}(n)\bigg\|_{\infty}^{2}\geq\epsilon(\delta).

By
[
38
, Lemma C.6]
(or rather the stronger Lemma
B.3
) and Fourier expanding via Lemma
A.6
we may assume that
\chi_{h}
is a product of
\mathbb{Z}
-multidegree nilcharacters. (We implicitly used that each vertical character
F
of the product can be taken to be a component of a nilcharacter of the same degree-rank. This follows from a slightly modified version of
[
38
, Lemma B.4]
, adapted to degree-rank. Applying that lemma, we find a nilcharacter
\chi
of the same
G_{(\ell+\ell^{\prime}-1,r)}
vertical frequency. Then by taking
(F/(2\|F\|_{\infty}),\chi\sqrt{1-|F/(2\|F\|_{\infty})|^{2}})
, we find that a multiple of
F
is a component of a nilcharacter.)

This implies that there exists at least
\epsilon(\delta)N^{k}
many
h\in[N]^{k}
such that
